**Of the 526 allocation rows in the register of American military aerospace designations, exactly 2 mention a sonic boom, and the second of them is this aeroplane** [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]]. Its entry is 84 characters long, which puts it twenty-fifth of the 30 X rows by length, and it reads in full that the aircraft **is a research aircraft that will create a shaped sonic boom signature**.

This is the sixtieth article in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the **The verb is the unusual thing.** The register's X rows say demonstrate 12 times, test 4 times, investigate 3 times, validate once and exhibit once. **The word create appears in 1 of the 526 rows, and this is the row.** Every other aeroplane in the series is written down as going to find something out about a phenomenon that already exists. This one is written down as going to make one.

**And the adjective is the programme.** The other sonic boom row is the [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54], allocated 5 May 2008, which was to be capable of generating relevant ground sonic boom signatures. **That aeroplane was never built.** The difference between the two sentences is the single word *shaped*, and the entire enterprise of the last sixty years of this subject is contained in it.

## What the Register Says, Which Is Very Little

The row was allocated 21 June 2018 to Lockheed Martin, sponsored by the National Aeronautics and Space Administration, with the engines cell left empty.

> *QueSST*; LBFD aircraft is a research aircraft that will create a shaped sonic boom signature.

**The empty engines cell means nothing and this series has now established that twice.** 88 of the register's 526 rows have no engine listed, among them the [X-57][related_post_a354_esaero_x57_maxwell] and the X-60A, and the [X-57][related_post_a354_esaero_x57_maxwell] recorded the false start in full so that later articles would not repeat it. **The [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] declined to repeat it and so does this one.**

**What is worth measuring is the length.** The mission statement of the [X-57][related_post_a354_esaero_x57_maxwell], allocated 25.6 months before this one, is 343 characters and is the longest in the X series. This one is 84. **The two neighbouring rows are the longest and the sixth shortest**, and the shorter of them describes a programme that cost the agency 247.5 million dollars at the contract award alone [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]].

**A register entry is not a measure of a programme.** That is the finding rather than a complaint, and the [X-57][related_post_a354_esaero_x57_maxwell] established the same thing from the other end by being the longest entry attached to an aeroplane that never flew.

### The Aeroplane Was Under Contract Before It Had a Number

NASA announced the contract to Lockheed Martin Aeronautics on 3 April 2018, stating that work began the previous day [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]]. **The designation was allocated 80 days later** [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]].

**This is the ordinary order of events and it is worth stating because the series has spent three articles on cases where it was not.** The [X-39][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned] was reserved and then lost because no letter was ever written. The [X-52][related_post_a349_x52_designation_refused] was requested in writing and refused. The [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] was skipped while it was still the next number due. **Here a contract was signed, an aeroplane was designed, and a number was applied for and granted in the normal way, and the interval was under three months.**

## What a Sonic Boom Is, Which Is Not What the Word Suggests

A body moving faster than sound cannot push the air out of its way in advance, because the news of its approach travels no faster than the air's own signalling speed. The disturbance therefore accumulates on a cone trailing the body, whose half-angle is fixed by the ratio of the two speeds.

$$
\sin\mu = \frac{1}{M}
\qquad\qquad
\cot\mu = \sqrt{M^2 - 1} = \beta
$$

At the design cruise Mach number of 1.4 the Mach angle is 45.6 degrees, so the cone is broad rather than needle-like [[Liepmann and Roshko, Elements of gasdynamics][book_liepmann]].

**The word boom suggests an explosion and the physics is nothing of the kind.** No energy is released. The aeroplane does work against the air continuously, and the pressure disturbance that work produces arrives at the ground all at once because the aeroplane outruns it. **A sonic boom is the sound of an aeroplane's steady flight delivered in the wrong order.**

### Whitham's Function, Which Is the Aeroplane Reduced to One Curve

The whole of linearised supersonic theory reduces an aircraft, for the purpose of what it sounds like far away, to a single function of one variable.

$$
\frac{\Delta p}{p_a} = \frac{\gamma M^2}{\sqrt{2\beta r}}\; F(\zeta)
$$

Here $F$ is Whitham's F-function, and the entire influence of shape, size, weight and attitude enters the far field through it [[Whitham 1952][research_whitham_1952]] [[Whitham, Linear and nonlinear waves][book_whitham]]. **A body's near field is complicated and its far field is one curve**, which is why sonic boom minimisation is a tractable design problem at all.

The F-function is obtained from the second derivative of an equivalent area distribution through an Abel transform.

$$
F(\zeta) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_0^{\zeta}
\frac{A_e''(x)}{\sqrt{\zeta - x}}\; \mathrm{d}x
$$

And the equivalent area is not the aeroplane's cross-section. It is the cross-section plus a term that accounts for lift [[Ashley and Landahl, Aerodynamics of wings and bodies][book_ashley_landahl]] [[Jones, Wing theory][book_jones]].

$$
A_e(x) = A_V(x) + \frac{\beta}{2q} \int_0^{x} L'(\xi)\; \mathrm{d}\xi
$$

**That second term is why a sonic boom cannot be designed away by making the aeroplane thin.** The aeroplane must hold itself up, and holding itself up means turning air downwards, and turning air downwards is indistinguishable at a distance from occupying volume. **A wing that carries weight has an equivalent area whether or not it has any thickness at all.**

The same equivalent area governs the wave drag through von Karman's double integral [[Kuchemann, The aerodynamic design of aircraft][book_kuchemann]] [[Area rule][ref_area_rule]].

$$
D_w = -\frac{q}{2\pi} \int_0^{\ell}\!\!\int_0^{\ell}
A_e''(x)\, A_e''(\xi)\, \ln|x - \xi| \; \mathrm{d}x \, \mathrm{d}\xi
$$

**So the loudness and the drag are computed from the same curve**, which is the most useful single fact in the subject and also the most constraining one. The two objectives are not independent, and neither are they aligned.

## Why the Boom Decays Faster Than Sound Does

A weak disturbance spreading from a line source in three dimensions has its amplitude fall with the square root of distance, because the energy is spread over a cylinder whose area grows linearly [[Pierce, Acoustics][book_pierce]].

$$
\Delta p \propto r^{-1/2}
$$

**A sonic boom does not obey that law.** Whitham's far-field result for a slender body gives a stronger decay, and a signature that grows longer as it weakens [[Whitham, Linear and nonlinear waves][book_whitham]] [[Maglieri and others, Sonic boom, six decades of research][book_maglieri]].

$$
\Delta p \propto r^{-3/4}
\qquad\qquad
T_s \propto r^{1/4}
$$

The discrepancy is not a correction. It is a physical process, and it can be found by asking where the energy has gone. The acoustic energy carried across unit length of a cylinder of radius $r$ goes as the circumference times the mean square pressure divided by the impedance times the duration of the wave.

$$
\Pi(r) \;\sim\; r\, \frac{(\Delta p)^2}{\rho c}\, T_s
\;\propto\; r \cdot r^{-3/2} \cdot r^{1/4} = r^{-1/4}
$$

**The energy falls as the minus one quarter power of the distance, so about 15.9 percent of it goes missing at every doubling, and it goes missing because the wave has shocks in it.** A shock is an irreversible compression. It converts ordered acoustic energy into heat, and it does so continuously as the wave propagates, and the classical linear result knows nothing about it.

**This is the reason the subject exists.** A signature that has coalesced into a shock-fronted N-wave is dissipating itself all the way to the ground, and arrives having converted a substantial part of its energy into a discontinuity that the human ear is exquisitely equipped to hear. **A signature that has not coalesced arrives quieter for two separate reasons, having lost less energy and having put what remains into frequencies the ear cares less about.** The design problem is to arrange the equivalent area so that coalescence does not complete within the propagation distance available.

**The propagation distance is not a free parameter.** It is the cruise altitude, and the cruise altitude is chosen by the engine and the wing.

## The Atmosphere Bends It, and There Is a Speed Below Which It Never Arrives

The atmosphere is not uniform. In the troposphere the temperature falls with altitude and the speed of sound falls with it, so a ray travelling downwards moves into progressively faster-signalling air and is refracted away from the vertical [[U.S. Standard Atmosphere, 1976][ref_us_standard_atmosphere]] [[Anderson, Modern compressible flow][book_anderson]].

$$
c(z) = \sqrt{\gamma R\,T(z)}
$$

$$
T(z) =
\begin{cases}
T_0 - \lambda z, & 0 \le z \le z_1 \\
T_1, & z_1 < z \le z_2
\end{cases}
$$

For a horizontally stratified medium the ray direction obeys an invariant of the same form as Snell's law in optics [[Pierce, Acoustics][book_pierce]].

$$
\frac{\cos\theta(z)}{c(z)} = \mathrm{constant}
$$

The consequence is a threshold. The horizontal trace speed of the wave along the ground equals the aircraft's own speed, and a wave whose trace speed is below the local speed of sound cannot propagate as a wave at that level at all. **So there is a Mach number below which the boom turns over in the air and never reaches the ground.**

$$
M_c = \frac{c(0)}{c(z_a)}
$$

That condition is standard in the sonic boom literature and is where the term Mach cutoff comes from [[Maglieri and others, Sonic boom, six decades of research][book_maglieri]]. Evaluated for the design cruise altitude of 55,000 feet in the standard atmosphere, which is 16,764 metres and lies in the isothermal layer above the tropopause at 216.65 kelvin, this gives the following.

$$
M_c = \frac{ 340.29 }{ 295.07 } = 1.153
\qquad\text{against}\qquad
M = 1.4
$$

**The design cruise Mach number is 21.4 percent above cutoff.** And the cutoff ratio has a maximum, because the atmosphere has a coldest layer. Sweeping the standard atmosphere from the ground to 30 kilometres, the largest value the ratio takes anywhere is 1.153. **So Mach 1.4 produces a boom that reaches the ground from every altitude, and no choice of cruise height would prevent it.**

**This number is not new to this series and the agreement is worth stating.** The [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54] derived it from the temperature ratio alone, since the speed of sound is the square root of the temperature and the rest cancels, and obtained the same 1.153 for the standard atmosphere above the tropopause. **Two articles, two derivations and one number.** That article went considerably further, computing where the ray turns over and showing how sharply the turning altitude collapses as the Mach number rises, and this one does not repeat that work.

### The Aeroplane Could Have Avoided the Problem Entirely and Refuses To

**A supersonic aeroplane that never wants to be heard has a simple option available to it.** Cruise below the cutoff Mach number. The boom refracts away, nobody on the ground hears anything, and no shaping is required. Proposals for supersonic business aircraft have been built on exactly this, under the name Mach cutoff cruise, and the [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54] established that signatures were measured in flight at cutoff Mach number and published in 1971.

**The X-59 rejects that solution, and the reason is that its product is the boom.** The mission is to fly over communities that have agreed to be flown over and to ask the people underneath what they heard. **An aeroplane that produces no measurable boom answers no question.** The programme needs a signature on the ground, of a known shape, at a known level, arriving at people who can be surveyed. Cutoff cruise would deliver silence, and silence is not evidence.

**That sets this aeroplane against the technique that has overtaken it.** The [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54]'s central finding was that the prohibition was ordered repealed on the strength of a technique that makes no ground boom at all rather than a quiet one, and that the technique is older than the prohibition. **The X-59 is the aeroplane built for the other answer**, and it flies 21.4 percent above the speed at which the problem would disappear, on purpose, because the other answer needs a boom to exist before anyone can be asked about it.

### The Published Speed Checks Out, Which Is Worth Doing

Multiplying the design Mach number by the speed of sound at the cruise altitude gives the following.

$$
V = M\, c(z_a) = 1.4 \times 295.07
= 413.1\ \mathrm{m/s} = 924.1\ \mathrm{mph}
$$

NASA has published 925 miles per hour for this aeroplane on its public pages and 940 miles per hour in the contract announcement [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]] [[Quesst, the Aircraft][ref_nasa_quesst_aircraft]]. **The first reproduces from Mach 1.4 at the cruise altitude to within 0.1 percent.** The second corresponds to Mach 1.42 at the same altitude, which is the figure that appears elsewhere as the design cruise Mach number.

**The two published speeds are therefore the same physical statement at two roundings**, which is a different result from the one the [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] reached, where two published speeds turned out to refer to two different altitudes. **The check is worth running precisely because it does not always pass.**

## Loudness Is Set by the Rise Time, Not by the Overpressure

A pressure signature is converted to a level on the decibel scale in the ordinary way [[Kinsler, Frey, Coppens and Sanders, Fundamentals of acoustics][book_kinsler]].

$$
L_p = 20 \log_{10} \frac{\Delta p}{p_r}
$$

**The overpressure a person experiences is not the one the aeroplane sent.** The ground is a nearly rigid boundary, so the incident wave and its reflection superpose and the pressure at a listener's ear is close to twice the incident value.

$$
\Delta p_g = K_R\, \Delta p_i
$$

A perfectly rigid surface gives exactly two, because the normal velocity must vanish there and the reflected wave arrives in phase with the incident one. **Real ground is not perfectly rigid**, so measurements give something under two, and 1.9 is the value this article uses. The reflection factor is not a constant, and it departs furthest from two exactly where the geometry is awkward, which is near the threshold Mach number and at the edge of the carpet [[Thomas 1971][research_thomas_1971]].

**Every published sonic boom overpressure in this article is a ground value and already contains this factor**, so the article never applies it twice. Read backwards, the 0.3 pounds per square foot published for this aeroplane implies an incident overpressure of 0.158 pounds per square foot before the ground doubled it, which is 7.6 pascals.

Concorde's boom, at the commonly published 2 pounds per square foot, is 95.8 pascals and therefore a peak sound pressure level of 133.6 decibels. The figure published for the X-59, 0.3 pounds per square foot, is 14.4 pascals and 117.1 decibels. **Those two numbers are almost useless.**

**They are almost useless because a boom is not a tone and the ear does not integrate it that way.** What the ear responds to in an impulsive event is the high-frequency content, and the high-frequency content of a shock-fronted wave is governed by how quickly the front rises. **Peak pressure sets how much energy arrives. Rise time sets where in the spectrum it lands.**

### What Classical Theory Says the Rise Time Should Be

A weak shock in a viscous, heat-conducting gas reaches a steady thickness when nonlinear steepening is balanced by diffusion. The balance is the classical Taylor result [[Hamilton and Blackstock, Nonlinear acoustics][book_hamilton_blackstock]].

$$
\delta = \frac{4\, \delta_d\, \rho_0\, c_0}{(\gamma + 1)\, \Delta p}
\qquad\qquad
\tau = \frac{\delta}{c_0}
$$

$$
\delta_d = \frac{1}{\rho_0}
\left[ \frac{4}{3}\eta + \eta_B
+ \kappa \left( \frac{1}{c_v} - \frac{1}{c_p} \right) \right]
$$

The bracket is the shear and bulk viscosities and the thermal conduction term together [[Volume viscosity][ref_bulk_viscosity]]. For air at sea level the diffusivity of sound is about 3.658 times ten to the minus five square metres a second. Evaluating the thickness for the overpressure published for this aeroplane gives 1.769 millimetres, and therefore a rise time as follows.

$$
\tau = 5.2\ \text{microseconds}
\qquad\text{against a measured}\qquad
\tau \approx 1\ \text{millisecond}
$$

**Classical theory is wrong here by a factor of about 192, which is 2.3 orders of magnitude.** Measured sonic boom rise times are of the order of a millisecond, not a microsecond, and the discrepancy has been known since booms were first recorded properly [[Maglieri and Sothcott 1990][research_maglieridomenicj_sothcottvictore_1990]] [[Cleveland and Blackstock 1994][research_cleveland_blackstock_1994]] [[Holst-Jensen 1981][research_holstjensen_1981]]. **It is not a small correction and it is not an experimental error.**

Two mechanisms are held responsible. The first is molecular relaxation. Nitrogen and oxygen molecules exchange energy between translational and vibrational modes on timescales far longer than the viscous one, that exchange is a much larger absorption than viscosity at the frequencies concerned, and it depends strongly on humidity [[Pierce 1970][research_pierce_1970]]. The second is atmospheric turbulence, which distorts the wavefront on the way down and smears the arrival in time [[Pierce 1971][research_pierce_1971]] [[Raspet et al 1994][research_raspetrichard_basshenrye_1994]]. **Both are real, both are documented, and the relative contribution of the two is not settled**, and at least one study concludes outright that molecular relaxation alone is insufficient to explain the observed shock structure [[Gionfriddo et al 1991][research_gionfriddo_kang_1991]].

**The consequence for this aeroplane is direct.** The rise time is the quantity that sets the loudness, the rise time is set by the atmosphere rather than by the airframe, and the atmosphere is not under the designer's control. **A predicted perceived level is therefore a prediction about the weather as much as about the aeroplane**, which is the reason the programme's second phase consists of flying over real communities in real weather rather than of computing harder.

### Why Rise Time Governs Loudness

Model the front of the signature as a smoothed step of rise time $\tau$. Its Fourier amplitude falls as one over frequency until the smoothing takes hold, and faster thereafter [[Pierce, Acoustics][book_pierce]] [[Pierce 1970][research_pierce_1970]].

$$
|P(f)| \propto \frac{1}{f \sqrt{1 + (2\pi f \tau)^2}}
$$

$$
f_c = \frac{1}{2\pi\tau}
$$

A rise time of one millisecond places the corner at 159.2 hertz. A rise time of ten milliseconds places it at 15.9 hertz. At 1,000 hertz, which is near the middle of the range where human hearing is most sensitive, the difference between those two signatures is 19.9 decibels.

**A factor of ten in rise time is worth twenty decibels where it matters, and costs nothing in peak pressure.** That is the whole of the argument for shaping stated in one line, and it is why a programme aimed at loudness spends its effort on the form of the waveform rather than on its height.

## Seventy-Five Is a Perception and Not a Pressure

The number the programme is built around is 75 on the Stevens Mark VII perceived level scale [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]] [[Doebler and Rathsam 2019][research_doebler_rathsam_2019]]. **That scale is a psychophysical construction and not an acoustic measurement**, and treating it as though it were a pressure produces mistakes that are widely repeated.

The Mark VII scale is defined so that loudness in sones doubles for a fixed increment of level [[Stevens 1972][research_stevens_1972]] [[Stevens, Psychophysics, introduction to its perceptual, neural and social prospects][book_stevens]] [[Sone][ref_sone]].

$$
PL = 40 + 32 \log_2 S
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
S = 2^{(PL - 40)/32}
$$

One sone is forty decibels of perceived level by construction, and a factor of two in loudness costs 32 decibels. Evaluating the two figures that are usually compared gives the following.

$$
S(75) = 2.134
\qquad\qquad
S(105) = 4.088
$$

**The programme's target is 47.8 percent quieter than Concorde in the units the scale is built to express** [[Fastl and Zwicker, Psychoacoustics, facts and models][book_zwicker_fastl]], not the ninety percent that is repeatedly stated. And the baseline is not agreed. Published figures for Concorde's perceived level include 102, 105 and 110, which give a range from 44.3 to 53.1 percent. **No reading of any published pair reaches ninety.**

$$
1 - \frac{S(75)}{S(105)} = 47.8\%
\qquad\text{against}\qquad
1 - 10^{-30/20} = 96.8\%
$$

### Where the Ninety Percent Comes From

The overpressures are 2 pounds per square foot against 0.3, and one minus that ratio is 85 percent. **That is the origin of the widely quoted figure, and it is a pressure ratio being reported as a reduction in loudness.**

**The two are not interchangeable and the difference is not small.** In pressure the reduction is 96.8 percent. In acoustic energy it is 99.9 percent. In perceived loudness, which is the quantity the target is stated in and the quantity a person underneath actually experiences, it is 47.8 percent. **Three defensible answers spanning fifty percentage points, and the one most often published is not any of them.**

This article does not claim the programme has overstated its case. **It claims that a figure quoted without its unit is not a claim at all**, and that the difference between halving a loudness and removing ninety-seven percent of a pressure is exactly the difference the Mark VII scale exists to express.

### How Much of the Reduction Is Shape

The claimed drop is 30 decibels of perceived level [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]]. If perceived level tracked peak overpressure the way sound pressure level does, the published overpressures alone would account for the following.

$$
\underbrace{PL_{\text{Concorde}} - PL_{\text{X-59}}}_{ 30 }
\;=\;
\underbrace{20\log_{10}\dfrac{\Delta p_i(\text{Concorde})}{\Delta p_i(\text{X-59})}}_{ 16.5 }
\;+\;
\underbrace{\text{what the shape contributes}}_{ 13.5 }
$$

**About 54.9 percent of the claimed reduction is amplitude and the rest is shape.** The amplitude term is 16.5 decibels and the residual is 13.5.

**This decomposition is this article's own construction and it rests on an assumption the sources do not make.** Perceived level is not a function of peak overpressure, which is the point of the preceding section, so treating the amplitude term as though it obeyed the decibel rule is a device for splitting the total rather than a physical statement. **It is stated here as inference and not as fact**, and what it establishes is a bound rather than a value. Even under an assumption that maximally favours amplitude, shape accounts for 13.5 decibels of the claim, and no amount of simply making the aeroplane quieter would have delivered it.

## The Shape That Costs, Measured

The obligation to spread the equivalent area smoothly over as long a distance as possible has a visible consequence, and it can be measured against the aeroplanes this one is compared with [[Concorde][ref_concorde]] [[Tupolev Tu-144][ref_tu144]] [[North American XB-70 Valkyrie][ref_xb70]] [[General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon][ref_f16]].

$$
\Lambda = \frac{\ell}{b}
$$

| Aircraft | Length over span |
|----------|------------------|
| X-59 | 3.36 |
| Concorde | 2.41 |
| Tupolev Tu-144 | 2.28 |
| North American XB-70 | 1.77 |
| General Dynamics F-16 | 1.51 |

**The X-59 is 1.39 times as slender as Concorde and more than twice as slender as a supersonic fighter.** It is ninety-nine feet long with a wingspan under thirty, and the proportion is not an aesthetic choice. It is the equivalent area distribution made visible. **A boom is shaped by making the aeroplane's disturbance grow gradually, and an aeroplane can only grow gradually if it has length to grow along.**

**And the length is not free.** A longer aeroplane has more wetted area for the same volume and lift, and therefore more skin friction drag, and a structure that must resist bending over a longer span of nothing. **The nose contributes no lift, carries no fuel, houses no payload and cannot be shortened**, which is a cost accepted in exchange for the only variable the design has.

### The Two Published Lengths Disagree

NASA's public description of the aeroplane gives its length as 99.7 feet [[Quesst, the Aircraft][ref_nasa_quesst_aircraft]]. The project's own technical overview, presented by the deputy project manager for technology, gives 99 feet [[X-59 Aircraft Overview and Status][ref_x59_overview]]. **The two differ by 0.7 feet and neither source is wrong in any interesting way**, the shorter being a rounded design parameter and the longer a measured overall dimension. It is recorded because this article states a slenderness ratio and the reader is entitled to know which figure it was computed from. **It was computed from the project's own, and the other would give 3.38.**

## The Aeroplane Is Mostly Other Aeroplanes

NASA's project overview annotates the configuration feature by feature, and the annotations divide cleanly into two kinds [[X-59 Aircraft Overview and Status][ref_x59_overview]].

**Four are about the boom.** A long nose to shape the forward shock. A T-tail to minimise the aft shock. Wing shielding to keep inlet spillage from spoiling the signature. A fixed canard for nose-up trim at the low-boom design point.

**Four are about cost and schedule, and say so in those words.** F-16 landing gear and other systems from high performance aircraft, to minimise qualification cost and schedule. A T-38 aft canopy and ejection seat, for the same stated reason. Large unitised skins, to reduce parts count and manufacturing cost. A single General Electric F414 engine with a standard nozzle, to minimise cost and schedule. A conventional tail arrangement is annotated separately, to simplify stability and control considerations.

**The aeroplane that exists to prove a shape is assembled, wherever the shape permits it, from parts chosen for not being new.** Contemporary accounts add a propulsion system component from the U-2 and a control stick from the F-117 to the list [[Lockheed Martin X-59 Quesst][ref_x59_wikipedia]].

**The same contractor has now had cost compression written into an X-plane's own register entry.** The [X-55][related_post_a352_lockheed_martin_x55] is described in the register as validating extreme time and cost compression in airframe manufacture using large unitised composite structures. **The X-59 uses large unitised skins for the stated reason of reducing parts count and manufacturing cost.** The register does not say so about this aeroplane and the project overview does, and both aeroplanes are Lockheed Martin's.

**Lockheed Martin holds 6 of the 30 X rows in the register and Boeing holds 12**, so the leading contractor in this series is not this one. **5 of Lockheed's 6 were allocated in 2009 or later**, which is where the recent concentration is.

## Seeing Out of an Aeroplane With No Forward Window

A nose long enough to shape a shock is a nose the pilot cannot see past. **The X-59 has no forward windscreen.** Forward vision is provided by the eXternal Vision System, which NASA describes as a 4K camera system feeding a processor and an ultra-high-definition display with integrated symbology [[X-59 Aircraft Overview and Status][ref_x59_overview]].

**The system was validated in flight before the aeroplane existed.** NASA installed X-59 hardware on a UC-12 and had guest pilots compare normal vision against the system on see-to-avoid and see-to-follow tasks [[X-59 Aircraft Overview and Status][ref_x59_overview]]. **A camera and a screen were qualified as a substitute for a window, on a different aeroplane, by pilots doing the tasks a window is for.**

**This is a considerable claim and it is worth stating plainly.** The aeroplane's forward vision is a rendering. The synthetic and enhanced vision literature that this substitution belongs to is thirty years deep and mostly about supplementing a view rather than replacing one, and this article's own survey had to be opened deliberately to reach it, which is recorded in the source base below.

## The Rule It Exists to Change Contains No Loudness at All

**The [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54] established the essential point five days ago and this article does not claim it.** The prohibition is a speed limit and not a noise limit, so a quiet aeroplane cannot satisfy it, and any aeroplane wanting to fly supersonic over land needs the rule replaced rather than met. **What this article adds is what the exemption path says**, because that is where a threshold would live if there were one.

The prohibition itself is one paragraph and it is worth reading rather than paraphrasing [[14 CFR 91.817][ref_far_91_817]].

> No person may operate a civil aircraft in the United States at a true flight Mach number greater than 1 except in compliance with conditions and limitations in an authorization to exceed Mach 1 issued to the operator in accordance with § 91.818.

**The rule bans a speed.** It does not ban a sound, it names no threshold, it contains no acoustic metric, and it makes no distinction between an aeroplane that produces a hundred and ten decibels at the ground and one that produces seventy-five.

The second paragraph does mention the boom, and what it requires is its absence.

> Information available to the flight crew includes flight limitations that ensure that flights entering or leaving the United States will not cause a sonic boom to reach the surface within the United States.

**Not a quiet boom. No boom.**

### The Exemption Is Written the Same Way

The authorisation process is the companion section. **It was added in its present form on 15 January 2021, which is 2.6 years after this aeroplane was designated and while it was being built** [[14 CFR 91.818][ref_far_91_818]]. It requires each applicant to state

> All conditions and limitations on the flight(s) that will ensure that no measurable sonic boom overpressure will reach the surface outside of the proposed flight area.

and it lists the purposes for which an authorisation may be granted at all. One of them is

> Establish a means of reducing or eliminating the effects of sonic boom, including flight profiles and special features of an aircraft.

**That clause describes this aeroplane exactly.** The X-59 is a special feature of an aircraft intended to reduce the effects of sonic boom, and the rule under which it flies names that as a purpose for which an authorisation may be granted. **Whether the wording predates the programme or was written with it in view is not settled by anything read here**, since the predecessor appendix this section replaced was not obtained, and the point is recorded as unresolved rather than assumed either way. Another clause permits flight outside a test area only on a conservative showing that no measurable overpressure reaches the surface. **Every path through the regulation is written around a boom that does not arrive.**

**So the regulatory situation is not that the X-59 will be quiet enough to satisfy the rule.** There is no number in the rule to satisfy. **The aeroplane's product is not compliance. It is evidence**, gathered so that a rulemaking might one day write a number where at present there is none, and the number would be new law rather than a threshold already waiting.

**This reframes what the register sentence is claiming.** An aeroplane that will create a shaped sonic boom signature is not an aeroplane built to pass a test. It is an instrument built to generate the data from which a test might be constructed. **The X-59 is a measuring device whose specimen is the public.**

### The Deadline Expired the Day Before This Article

**Executive Order 14304 of 6 June 2025 directed the Federal Aviation Administration to repeal the prohibition within 180 days and to establish an interim noise-based certification standard in its place** [[Executive Order 14304][ref_eo_14304]] [[Executive Order 14304, American Presidency Project][ref_eo_14304_apps]]. The [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54], written 5 days ago, recorded that the deadline falls on 3 December 2025 and said plainly that what happened on it was outside what that article could report.

**And the order does not contain a number either.** It directs that an interim noise-based certification standard be established and does not say what it should be, leaving the threshold to a notice of proposed rulemaking it requires within eighteen months. **So the rule names no loudness, the exemption path requires no boom at all, and the order that would replace the rule declines to say what loudness would be acceptable.** Three documents, and the number the aeroplane was built to justify appears in none of them.

**This article is dated 1 day after that deadline, and what happened is that nothing did.** The text of both sections quoted above carries no amendment later than 15 January 2021, so the prohibition stood unchanged on the day the order required it to be gone. **No repeal, no interim standard, and no number.**

**That is reported as a state of the record and not as a prediction.** A rulemaking is a slow instrument and an executive deadline is not a statute, so an expired deadline is an ordinary event rather than a scandal. **What matters here is narrower.** The aeroplane's entire purpose is to supply evidence for a standard, the order that demanded the standard has come and gone without one, and **at the editorial date there is still nothing in the regulation for a quiet aeroplane to be quiet enough for.**

### And the Metric Itself Is Not in the Rule Either

Perceived level is not named anywhere in either section. The federal aviation noise standards for subsonic aircraft are written in effective perceived noise decibels, a different metric with a different weighting and a different history, developed for the sustained noise of a jet transport passing overhead rather than for an impulse lasting a fraction of a second [[SAE ARP865][ref_sae_arp865]] [[14 CFR Part 36][ref_far_part_36]].

**A rulemaking that adopted the programme's number would be adopting a metric as well as a threshold**, and the metric has been shown to correlate with human response to booms specifically, which is the reason the programme uses it. **That correlation is itself a research result rather than a regulatory fact**, and it is one of the things the community phase is designed to test.

## Range and Thrust, Displayed and Not Evaluated

The cruise performance of the aeroplane follows the ordinary relations [[Raymer, Aircraft design, a conceptual approach][book_raymer]] [[Range (aeronautics)][ref_breguet_range]].

$$
R_c = \frac{M\, c}{c_t}\; \frac{L}{D}\; \ln \frac{W_i}{W_f}
$$

$$
\frac{F_N}{F_{N0}} = \left( \frac{\rho}{\rho_0} \right)^{n}
$$

**Neither is evaluated in this article and the reason is that the inputs are not published.** The X-59's weight, its fuel fraction, its lift-to-drag ratio at the design condition and the installed thrust of its engine at 55,000 feet are not stated in any source consulted. **A range figure could be produced by assuming three of them, and it would be a statement about the assumptions.**

**This is a boundary and it is left standing deliberately.** The [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] established that an aeroplane's published range can be used to bound its lift-to-drag ratio when the range is published. Here it is not. **The relations are displayed because they define the quantities the following section says are missing**, and for no other reason.

**One thing the relations do settle without numbers.** The aeroplane is not required to go far. Its mission is to fly a measured pass over an instrumented area or a surveyed community and return, so range is not a design driver, and the slenderness that costs it skin friction costs it something it does not need. **The design trade is unusually clean because the objective is unusually narrow.**

## At the Editorial Date It Had Flown Once

The X-59 first flew on 28 October 2025, taking off from Lockheed Martin's Palmdale facility and landing at NASA Armstrong at Edwards [[X-59 First Flight][ref_nasa_first_flight]]. **The flight lasted 67 minutes, reached about 12,000 feet and about 230 miles an hour, and the landing gear stayed down throughout.**

**That is 24.9 percent of the design cruise speed, at 21.8 percent of the design cruise altitude, with the gear extended.** As of this article's date, 37 days later, **the aeroplane had made 1 flight and had never been supersonic.**

**Everything the register says about it is therefore still in the future tense, and the register wrote it in the future tense.** The aeroplane that will create a shaped sonic boom signature has not yet created one. It has not yet made any sonic boom at all.

### Against the Schedule the Programme Published

NASA's contract announcement stated that work ran through 31 December 2021, that the agency anticipated accepting the aeroplane in late 2021, and that flights over selected United States cities would begin in mid-2022 [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]].

**First flight came 3.82 years after the stated end of the contract**, 7.35 years after the designation was allocated, and 655 days after the aeroplane was rolled out. **The community overflight phase, which was to begin in mid-2022, had not begun.**

**This is recorded as a fact about the schedule and not as a judgement about the programme.** A one-off research aeroplane with a novel configuration, no forward windscreen and a single-string flight-critical vision system is not a schedule-predictable object, and the series has now documented several. **What matters for this article is narrower.** The central claim in the register, and the central claim of the programme, is a claim about a pressure signature at the ground, **and at the editorial date not one such signature had been produced.**

## Symbols

**Every symbol used in a display equation above is declared here, and every symbol declared here is used.** That is checked mechanically rather than asserted, and the check earned its keep on this article by refusing an environment name that a one-letter symbol had eaten. **It reported the residue of the word cases**, left behind after the declared symbol for the speed of sound was removed from inside it, which is the exact failure the scanner carries a warning about in its own source.

| Symbol | Meaning |
|--------|---------|
| $A_V$ | the part of the equivalent area contributed by volume alone |
| $A_e$ | the equivalent area distribution |
| $D$ | the drag force |
| $D_w$ | the wave drag |
| $F$ | Whitham's F-function, the aircraft's acoustic signature at the source |
| $F_N$ | the installed net thrust |
| $F_{N0}$ | the installed net thrust at sea level and the same Mach number |
| $K_R$ | the ground reflection factor |
| $L$ | the lift force |
| $L'$ | the lift per unit length along the aircraft |
| $L_p$ | the sound pressure level |
| $M$ | the flight Mach number |
| $M_c$ | the cutoff Mach number, below which no boom reaches the ground |
| $P$ | the Fourier amplitude of the pressure signature |
| $PL$ | the Stevens Mark VII perceived level, in decibels |
| $R$ | the specific gas constant of air |
| $R_c$ | the still-air cruise range |
| $S$ | the total loudness of the signature, in sones |
| $T$ | the absolute air temperature |
| $T_0$ | the sea-level standard temperature |
| $T_1$ | the temperature of the isothermal layer above the tropopause |
| $T_s$ | the duration of the ground signature |
| $V$ | the true airspeed |
| $W_f$ | the aircraft weight at the end of cruise |
| $W_i$ | the aircraft weight at the start of cruise |
| $\Delta p$ | the peak overpressure of the pressure signature |
| $\Delta p_g$ | the overpressure at the ground after reflection |
| $\Delta p_i$ | the overpressure incident on the ground before reflection |
| $\Lambda$ | the slenderness ratio, overall length divided by span |
| $\Pi$ | the acoustic energy per unit length of a cylinder of radius $r$ |
| $\beta$ | the Prandtl-Glauert parameter, $\sqrt{M^2-1}$ |
| $\delta$ | the thickness of a steady weak shock |
| $\delta_d$ | the diffusivity of sound in air |
| $\ell$ | the overall length of the aircraft |
| $\eta$ | the shear viscosity of air |
| $\eta_B$ | the bulk viscosity of air |
| $\gamma$ | the ratio of specific heats of air |
| $\kappa$ | the thermal conductivity of air |
| $\lambda$ | the tropospheric temperature lapse rate |

| $\rho$ | the local air density |
| $\rho_0$ | the air density at sea level |
| $\tau$ | the rise time of the front shock |
| $\theta$ | the elevation angle of an acoustic ray above the horizontal |
| $\xi$ | a dummy variable of integration in $x$ |
| $\zeta$ | the retarded streamwise coordinate in which the F-function is written |
| $b$ | the wing span |

| $n$ | the exponent of the thrust lapse with density |

### The aeroplane, the programme and the aeroplanes it descends from

**This is the cluster that names things**, and it holds the demonstrator, its two flying ancestors and the three generations of unbuilt supersonic transport that produced the theory.

## The Source Base

**48 hand-written references carry the argument and 4,101 harvested records map the field, and this article keeps them apart on purpose.** The claims about what a document does and does not contain rest on five documents read in full, being the register, the two sections of the federal aviation regulations, the executive order directing their repeal, and the programme's own conference presentation. **Those are not commentary on the evidence. They are the evidence.**

### The Pool

**Three sweeps retrieved 16,217 records, being 7,788, 2,872, 5,557, of which 14,831 were distinct.** The subject gate admitted 4,293 of those, 192 fell to duplicate registration, and 4,101 remain in the reference list.

**The report servers behave nothing like each other and the difference is large enough to change how a sweep should be built.** The space agency's server returned 399 records to 93 questions, or 4.3 a question. The defence registry returned 3,834 to 30, or 127.8 a question. **The first is capped and the second is not**, so the first is bought with questions and the second with patience.

### The Report-Primary Fraction

**The report-primary fraction is 13.9 percent, being 571 records of 4,101.** The two report servers were asked 93 and 30 questions across 3 sweeps.

**The two preceding articles in this series whose subjects were administrative acts measured 0.0 percent and 2.0 percent, the two lowest of the series** [[X-Planes: X-39, Reserved but Never Assigned][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned]] [[X-Planes: X-52, the Designation Refused][related_post_a349_x52_designation_refused]]. **This article's subject is a research programme run by a research agency**, and the agency publishes its own reports, so a high fraction here would be unremarkable and a low one would need explaining.

**The ceiling was bought off in the draft pass rather than in the third.** The [X-57][related_post_a354_esaero_x57_maxwell] measured that the space agency's server reports its true total and serves ten records however the query is phrased, and ignores the offset. The [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] acted on that finding for the first time, in its third pass, asking 42 questions where its draft had asked 12. **This article's first sweep asked 42 and its second asked 51**, which is the same purchase made before the defect rather than after it. The server returned 399 records in all, or 4.3 a question.

### The Store Families That Were Opened, and Why There Are So Many

**13 of the store's 28 tagged families are open for this article.** That is the largest number any article in this series has opened, and the reason is structural rather than accidental. **The store was built by an aeronautical series, and its noise families were tagged as contaminants because for those articles they were contaminants.** Wind turbine noise annoyance, railway noise annoyance and the health literature of aircraft noise were correctly refused by fifty-nine previous articles. **This article's subject is a sound.**

**Opening them releases 228 records that the gate then keeps.** Measured one family at a time the releases sum to 217, which is fewer than the joint figure by 11. The largest single contributor is `hypersonics` at 65.

**The sign of that difference is the interesting part and it is the opposite of what the two previous measurements found.** A353 and A354 both found the parts summing to more than the whole, at two records and thirty-one, because a record held by two armed patterns in the same family is counted twice. **Two effects are in play and only one of them was ever measured.** A record held by patterns from two *different* families is released only when both are open, so it appears in neither single-family measurement and does appear in the joint one. **With 13 families open the second effect dominates**, by 11 records, and the whole exceeds the sum of its parts.

**Two families were kept armed on a distinction worth stating.** `dentistry` and `electric-machines` each release exactly one record, and each of those is a psychoacoustic characterisation of a machine. **A psychoacoustic metric is shared between this subject and those, and a community dose-response study is not**, and the two noise families above were opened for the second kind with the first riding along. These hold only the first kind.

### A Place Name Was Deleting the Measurement Literature

**A missile range is where a sonic boom is measured.** The `missiles` family, whose own note in the store says it must not be carried blindly, was removing the White Sands Missile Range sonic boom propagation experiment and the measurements of booms from air combat training conducted there. **The words that matched are in a place name.** The family was opened, and the supersonic slender-body aerodynamics it also holds is this article's method rather than its contamination.

### An Untagged Family Cannot Be Opened, and the X-59 Has No Forward Window

**The pool holds 99 titles about synthetic vision, head-up displays, cockpit displays and symbology, and the store was deleting them.** The family was written by A347 against the human-factors literature of rotorcraft displays, where it is genuinely about the pilot of a helicopter and A347's subject was unmanned. **It carried no tag, so there was no handle to open it by.**

**A354 recorded that a family must be tagged before it can be opened and that some are not. This is the next case.** The repair is a tag rather than a weakening, on the A352 adhesive-bonding precedent, because hover symbology scaling is still contamination for the survey the family was written for. Opening it releases 16 records through the gate, and they are this aeroplane's own subject, because the X-59 is flown forward on a camera.

### A Word Inserted Into a Multi-Word Anchor Defeats It

**The gate said `perceived level` and the founding document of the metric family says `perceived noise level`.** So `Definitions and Procedures for Computing the Perceived Noise Level of Aircraft Noise` was refused for carrying no subject anchor, by an article whose central distinction is between two perceived-level metrics.

**A355 was defeated twice by a trailing letter s and recorded it. This is the same failure in another grammatical costume**, an inserted word rather than a plural, and it was found the same way, by reading the dropped sample rather than by reasoning about the pattern. The anchor now admits the inserted word and the abbreviations beside it, and the repair is asserted against the record that found it.

### The Two Homonyms This Subject Was Always Going to Meet

**Sonic hedgehog is one of the most cited genes in developmental biology, and the pool holds 14 titles naming it, of which 0 reached the reference list.** All 14 were refused, which is the evidence that the guard was needed rather than merely prudent. **`sonic` was never queried or gated bare and the guard was written into the gate before the first run**, which is the [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] lesson applied in the right place rather than in the store.

**The word boom is worse.** The pool holds 1,246 titles containing it and 1,009 containing the phrase sonic boom. An economic boom, a baby boom, a crane boom, a refuelling boom and an oil containment boom are all boom, and the last of those arrived as `Oil Spill Boom Computational Fluid Dynamics and Physical Modeling` and was refused. **Every anchor requires a physics word beside the boom.**

**And `signature` is the quiet third one.** The pool holds 100 titles containing it, of which 37 have an acoustic or pressure word beside it. **The register's own sentence about this aeroplane uses the word**, and a gene expression signature, a digital signature, a radar signature and a mutational signature are all signature.

### The Word the Programme Uses Is Not the Word the Field Uses

**The programme says sonic thump and 3 titles in 14,831 agree, against 1,009 saying sonic boom.** The [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] found the same shape with attritable against low cost. **A programme's chosen word is a communications decision and the literature keeps its own**, so the gate anchors on both and the article uses the field's word except where it is quoting.

### The Conclusions Were Probed, and One Was Harvested For

**9 of 10 conclusions measure thin under this article's own words and 10 of 10 are covered under the field's.** That is the fifth article running with that result, and the reason is the same. **A probe written in the author's phrasing measures the author.**

**One conclusion stayed thin after rewording and it was harvested for.** The claim that a shock-fronted N-wave decays faster than geometric spreading because it dissipates at its own shocks measured 9 titles under the field's words in a pool of 9,851. **That is a fact about the questions asked.** The first two sweeps queried the boom by its aeronautical names, and this half of the subject lives in nonlinear acoustics under different ones. A third sweep of 42 queries in the other field's vocabulary took the claim to 148.

**And one thing the harvest did not change is worth recording, because it surprised this article.** The string `N-wave` appears in 0 of the 14,831 titles harvested, and it appeared in 0 of the 9,851 before the sweep aimed at it. **The name this article uses for the object is not a name the literature puts in titles**, and a probe written on it would have measured the author rather than the field however many sweeps were run.

**Nothing remains thin after the harvest**, which is the first time in this series that has been true.

### A Corpus Check Has Been Silently Inactive for Sixty Articles

**The corpus verifier gates a survey cluster row against its own citation count, and it has never once fired on this series.** The rule matches a line beginning with the record count in bold and carrying the citations on the same line, which is the format four earlier articles used and the format sixty-six rows were measured in before the check was promoted. **Every article of the X-Planes series has written the count inside a sentence with the citations on the line below**, which matches nothing.

**Nothing was ungated, because each article's own verifier recomputes the same agreement from the reference data, and this one does too. But a check that cannot fire is not a check**, and the emitted rows in this article are written in the shape the corpus rule can read, so from here the gate applies to the series as well as to the article.

### Where the Displayed Relations Come From

**Every relation displayed in this article was evaluated before it was displayed, except the two that could not be**, and those two are named in the epistemic state as boundaries rather than presented as results. The symbol table is generated from the same declaration the equations are checked against, and a symbol declared twice is a build failure rather than a reader's confusion.

### A Source-Level Linter Reads a Tall Bracket as a Broken Link

**The corpus linter reports two split link pairs in this article and both are the same display equation.** A tall bracket written as `\left[` on one source line and `\right]` on another looks, to a scanner reading markdown, like a reference link opened on one line and closed on the next.

**It is recorded rather than worked around.** The finding is at convention severity and fails nothing, the rendered audit sees the built page and reports no findings on it, and this corpus has already established that the source-level linter once reported 1,596 defect-severity findings that the rendered pages did not carry. **Contorting the mathematics to satisfy a heuristic that cannot see the page would be the wrong trade.**

### The Regulations Site Serves a Stub to an Automated Fetcher

**An HTTP 200 is not a verified citation, and this article met the sharpest form of that yet.** The electronic code of federal regulations answers this corpus's fetching library with about ten kilobytes of navigation chrome for a section that is eighty kilobytes in a browser, and **the clause the article quotes is not in the ten**. A checker that asked only whether the address returned a document would have passed it, and a checker that asked whether the document contained the quoted clause would have failed a link that is perfectly good.

**Both sections were therefore retrieved with a browser user agent, saved, and quoted from the saved copy**, and the article's verifier checks every regulatory block quote against that copy and then checks the copy against the live page. The federal register site refuses an automated fetch outright and redirects to an unblock page, so the executive order was read from a university archive of the same text and both addresses are cited.

### One Catalogue Record Misspells Its Own Title

**The library index records Maglieri's 2014 survey of the field as `Soinc boom`.** The reference list gives the title the work actually has. The discrepancy is recorded here rather than smoothed over, because a reader following the link will see the misspelling and is entitled to know it was noticed.

## Epistemic State

### Historical Fact

The X-59A designation was allocated on 21 June 2018 to Lockheed Martin with NASA as sponsor, and its mission statement in the register is the one quoted [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]]. NASA announced the design and build contract on 3 April 2018 at 247.5 million dollars, stating a period of performance ending 31 December 2021 and an intention to fly over selected United States cities beginning in mid-2022 [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]]. The aeroplane first flew on 28 October 2025 for 67 minutes, reaching approximately 12,000 feet and 230 miles per hour with the landing gear down, from Palmdale to Edwards [[X-59 First Flight][ref_nasa_first_flight]]. The design cruise condition is Mach 1.4 at 55,000 feet and the design target is 75 on the Stevens Mark VII perceived level scale [[NASA Release 18-020][ref_nasa_release_18_020]] [[Doebler and Rathsam 2019][research_doebler_rathsam_2019]]. Executive Order 14304 of 6 June 2025 directs the repeal of the prohibition within 180 days and the establishment of an interim noise-based certification standard, without naming a threshold [[Executive Order 14304, American Presidency Project][ref_eo_14304_apps]]. **The deadline fell on 3 December 2025 and the sections quoted in this article carry no amendment later than 15 January 2021.**

### Recomputed From the Register by This Article

The register carries 526 allocation rows of which 30 are X designations. **2 rows of the 526 mention a sonic boom and they are the X-54A and the X-59A.** **The word create appears in 1 row.** The X-59A mission statement is 84 characters against a median of 119 for the X rows and 343 for its immediate predecessor, placing it twenty-fifth of 30 by length. 88 rows have an empty engines cell. Lockheed Martin holds 6 X rows and NASA sponsors 10. **Every one of these was recomputed from the register file by this article's verifier and none was taken from a secondary source.**

### Verified by Reading the Document

The text of 14 CFR 91.817 and 14 CFR 91.818 was retrieved and read in full, and the passages quoted are quoted from what was read [[14 CFR 91.817][ref_far_91_817]] [[14 CFR 91.818][ref_far_91_818]]. **Neither section contains a loudness threshold, an acoustic metric, or any number describing a permissible sonic boom.** The design feature annotations attributed to NASA were read from the project's own conference presentation [[Richwine, X-59 Aircraft Overview and Status][ref_x59_overview]].

### Analysis, Which Is Arithmetic on Published Inputs

The cutoff Mach number of 1.153 at the cruise altitude, and the finding that the cutoff ratio nowhere exceeds 1.153 in the standard atmosphere. The reproduction of the published 925 miles per hour to within 0.1 percent, and the identification of the released 940 miles per hour as Mach 1.42 at the same altitude. The conversion of 75 and 105 perceived level decibels to 2.134 and 4.088 sones and the resulting 47.8 percent. The classical Taylor rise time of 5.2 microseconds. The 19.9 decibel benefit of a tenfold rise time at 1,000 hertz. The slenderness ratios in the table. All are computed in this article's own calculation module from the published inputs named beside them.

### Inference, Which Is This Article's Own and Is Labelled

**That the decomposition of the claimed 30 decibel reduction into 16.5 decibels of amplitude and 13.5 of shape is a bound rather than a measurement.** It assumes that perceived level would track peak overpressure by the decibel rule, which the preceding sections establish is false, and it is used only to argue that shape cannot account for less than the residual.

**That the register's use of the verb create marks this row as different in kind.** The count of one is a fact. The reading that a shaped signature is a thing made rather than a phenomenon studied is this article's own, and nobody has been found stating it.

**That the aeroplane's configuration is dominated by cost and schedule wherever the boom does not constrain it.** The annotations are NASA's and the tally is this article's.

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**Whether the aeroplane will meet 75 perceived level decibels in flight.** At the editorial date it had flown 1 time, subsonically, and no shaped signature had been produced or measured.

**The relative contribution of molecular relaxation and atmospheric turbulence to the observed rise time.** Both are documented, and the sources consulted do not agree on the split.

**Concorde's perceived level.** Published values include 102, 105 and 110, and this article computes its headline comparison at 105 while giving the range the alternatives produce.

**Whether 0.3 pounds per square foot is a design prediction, a computed nominal or a specification.** It is widely quoted and the sources consulted do not attribute it to a NASA document with a stated basis.

**What form any replacement standard will take.** Executive Order 14304 requires an interim noise-based certification standard and does not say whether it would be written as a perceived level, an overpressure, or something else, and at the editorial date no proposed rule had appeared. **A standard written as an overpressure would not use the metric this programme has spent a decade calibrating**, and nothing read here settles which it will be.

**The aeroplane's weight, fuel fraction, lift-to-drag ratio and installed thrust**, none of which is published, which is why the range and thrust relations are displayed and never evaluated.

## Out of Scope

**The engineering of the eXternal Vision System beyond its existence and its validation.** It is a substantial subject and it is a display and human factors subject rather than an aerodynamic one.

**The economics of supersonic passenger transport.** Whether a market exists for a quieter supersonic airliner is a question about airlines and not about shock waves, and this article makes no claim either way.

**The environmental case beyond noise.** Fuel burn per passenger kilometre and stratospheric emissions are the other two objections to supersonic transport, both serious, and neither is what this aeroplane addresses.

**Every X-plane after the X-59.** The X-60A and beyond belong to later articles in this series.

## Conclusion

**The register gives this aeroplane one sentence and the sentence contains the whole programme in one adjective.** An aeroplane that will create a shaped sonic boom signature. The other of the register's two sonic boom rows, the [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54], was to generate relevant ground signatures and was never built. **The difference between generating a signature and shaping one is the difference between measuring a phenomenon and manufacturing one**, and the register's verb, used once in 526 rows, marks it.

**The physics supports the ambition.** A signature that has not coalesced into a shock-fronted wave loses less energy on the way down and puts what remains at frequencies the ear discounts, and a factor of ten in rise time is worth about 19.9 decibels where hearing is sharpest. **The design problem is real, the mechanism is understood, and the aeroplane's extraordinary proportions are that mechanism made visible.**

**Three things are less settled than the programme's public arithmetic suggests.**

**The claimed reduction is not ninety percent in any unit the target is stated in.** In perceived loudness it is 47.8 percent against the most commonly published baseline, and between 44.3 and 53.1 percent across every baseline this article found. In pressure it is 96.8 percent and in energy 99.9. **The 85 percent that the published overpressures give is a pressure ratio being reported as a loudness.** The Mark VII scale exists precisely to prevent that substitution, and 30 decibels of perceived level is a little less than halving the loudness, because halving it takes 32.

**The baseline is part of the uncertainty and this article does not pretend otherwise.** Concorde's perceived level is published at 102, 105 and 110, the sources consulted do not agree, and the article computes its headline figure at 105 and shows what the alternatives give.

**The quantity that governs the answer is not under the designer's control.** Loudness is set by rise time, classical theory underestimates rise time by more than two orders of magnitude, and what makes up the difference is molecular relaxation and turbulence in the atmosphere on the day. **A predicted perceived level is a prediction about the weather**, which is why the programme's second phase is to fly over real communities rather than to compute harder.

**And the rule the aeroplane exists to change contains no loudness at all.** The [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54] established that it is a speed limit rather than a noise limit. This article adds that its exemption path requires not a quiet boom but no measurable boom, and that the executive order directing its repeal declines to name a threshold and leaves that to a later rulemaking. **There is no number in any of the three documents**, and the aeroplane's output is therefore not compliance but evidence, gathered so that a number might one day be written where none exists.

**The deadline for writing it expired the day before this article.** The order of 6 June 2025 gave 180 days, the 3 December 2025 deadline came, and the regulation stood unchanged. **That is not a judgement about the programme or the agency**, since a rulemaking is slow and an executive deadline is not a statute. It is the state of the record at the date on this article, and it is the first date in this series at which the record could be read at all.

**At the editorial date the aeroplane had flown once, for 67 minutes, at 24.9 percent of its design cruise speed, with the landing gear down.** It had never been supersonic. The contract that built it named 31 December 2021 and the flights over cities were to begin in mid-2022. **Everything the register says about this aeroplane is written in the future tense, and at the time of writing the future tense was still correct.**
